# Phase 3C Engineering Architecture and Prototype Strategy for VibeGuard

## 1. Executive Summary

VibeGuard aims to demonstrate that a low-cost, standalone embedded system can measure machine vibration locally, learn or recognize a normal operating condition, detect deliberately introduced abnormal vibration, classify states as Normal or Abnormal, and indicate the result on-device without cloud dependence. Phase 3B concluded that the engineering hypothesis is technically feasible for the assigned student team within a ₹3000–₹5000 budget and semester timeline, with single-node vibration discrimination identified as the hardest open problem. This Phase 3C report selects and justifies a concrete architecture centered on a single MEMS accelerometer, an ESP32-class MCU, time-domain and simple spectral features, and threshold/statistical decision logic, with optional TinyML as a stretch goal.[^1][^2][^3]

The recommended prototype uses a digital 3-axis MEMS accelerometer rigidly mounted on a small lab machine or rig, sampled at 1–5 kHz by an ESP32; vibration segments are processed into RMS, peak, standard deviation, and band-limited spectral energy, then classified by calibrated thresholds or a lightweight anomaly detector. The system operates entirely at the edge, logs basic data for debugging, and drives LEDs or a small display to indicate Normal/Abnormal. This architecture balances engineering validity, buildability by three CS/Cybersecurity-focused students, low mechanical complexity, and strong educational value while leaving room for future upgrades.[^4][^5]

***

## 2. Architecture Investigation

### 2.1 Candidate Architecture Classes

Phase 3A and 3B established that industrial condition monitoring commonly uses piezoelectric accelerometers, velocity sensors, and MEMS accelerometers for vibration measurement, with standards such as ISO 10816/20816 governing evaluation on non-rotating parts. Piezoelectric accelerometers offer very wide frequency range and long-term stability and are the de facto standard in high-end predictive maintenance, while MEMS accelerometers have matured enough to be used for condition monitoring in smaller machines.[^6][^7][^5][^4]

For VibeGuard’s student, low-cost, edge-intelligence context, the following architecture families are relevant:

- Single MEMS accelerometer + MCU (digital sensor, moderate bandwidth, simple wiring).
- Piezoelectric accelerometer + analog front-end + MCU (analog sensor, higher bandwidth, more complex electronics).[^4]
- Multi-sensor arrays (multiple accelerometers or mixed sensors on different machine locations).
- Hybrid systems combining analog front-end, digital sensors, and more advanced signal processing.

### 2.2 Comparative Assessment

Industrial piezoelectric accelerometers typically provide usable bandwidth up to 10–20 kHz and superior dynamic range and long-term stability, but require specialized signal conditioning (IEPE or 4–20 mA loops) and are relatively expensive. MEMS accelerometers for condition monitoring, by contrast, are lower-cost, can integrate directly with microcontrollers via SPI/I²C, and come in compact packages suitable for PCB-level integration, but trade off bandwidth and resolution, with typical usable ranges around 2–3 kHz for low-cost parts.[^8][^9][^10][^6][^4]

Multi-sensor architectures can help spatial noise isolation and fault localization but increase cost, mechanical complexity, cabling, and signal-processing burden beyond the project’s student scope and budget. Edge-AI-heavy architectures (dense neural networks, complex anomaly detection) similarly add computational and data-collection complexity that Phase 3B identified as unnecessary for minimum demonstrable success.[^5][^4]

Given VibeGuard’s mission—detect deliberately introduced abnormal vibration on an accessible lab machine rather than industrial turbines—the single-sensor MEMS+MCU architecture offers the best trade-off between feasibility, cost, and educational value.[^2][^3][^6]

***

## 3. Sensor Evaluation

### 3.1 Sensor Classes

Relevant sensor classes include:

- Piezoelectric accelerometers (PE) for high-frequency, high-dynamic-range monitoring.[^8][^4]
- Piezovelocity sensors with integrated velocity output tailored for low-frequency rotating machinery.[^11][^12]
- MEMS accelerometers (variable capacitance) for compact, low-cost vibration sensing.[^6][^4]
- Industrial loop-powered vibration sensors (4–20 mA) for direct PLC integration.[^9][^8]

### 3.2 Operating Principles and Characteristics

Piezoelectric accelerometers generate charge proportional to applied acceleration using PZT ceramics, offering wide temperature range, broad dynamic range, and bandwidth exceeding 10 kHz, with no DC response and minimal zero drift. MEMS variable-capacitance accelerometers measure acceleration via changes in capacitance of a seismic mass between capacitor plates and require integrated electronics to convert tiny capacitance changes to voltage or digital output; they offer lower bandwidth and sometimes higher noise but are cheaper and easier to integrate with embedded systems.[^5][^4][^6]

Industry white papers comparing PE and MEMS accelerometers conclude that PE sensors remain superior for high-end industrial predictive maintenance due to their wide frequency response and long-term stability, but MEMS devices are increasingly viable for condition monitoring where ultra-high bandwidth is not required and cost and integration simplicity matter.[^4][^6]

### 3.3 Comparative Table

| Attribute | Piezoelectric accelerometer | MEMS accelerometer |
|----------|-----------------------------|--------------------|
| Frequency response | Up to >10–20 kHz typical usable >10 kHz[^4][^8] | Usable bandwidth ~2–3 kHz for many VC MEMS designs[^4][^6] |
| Sensitivity & noise | High dynamic range, low noise, suitable for fine diagnostics[^4] | Higher noise, lower resolution, adequate for gross condition monitoring[^4][^6] |
| Long-term drift | Sensitivity drift only; no DC drift[^4] | Zero and sensitivity drift possible over time[^4] |
| Interface complexity | Requires constant-current IEPE or charge amps; analog cabling[^8][^4] | SPI/I²C digital or analog voltage, simple PCB-level integration[^6][^10] |
| Cost | Industrial-grade units often expensive relative to student budgets[^8][^13] | Commodity MEMS accelerometers inexpensive and widely available[^6][^10] |
| Mounting options | Stud/bolt mounting for robust coupling; heavier housings[^5][^14] | Small SMT or module designs; require careful mechanical mounting for good coupling[^6][^15] |

### 3.4 Recommended Sensors

**Technically strongest option:** An industrial piezoelectric accelerometer with appropriate frequency range, such as devices conforming to IEPE standards, would maximize sensing fidelity and best reflect industrial practice, but its cost and interface complexity exceed typical student project constraints.[^13][^8][^4]

**Practical student-project option:** A digital MEMS accelerometer (e.g., multi-axis SPI/I²C accelerometer modules used for condition monitoring) offers sufficient bandwidth to capture key fault-related vibration components for small motors and lab rigs and integrates directly with common MCUs, making it the preferred choice for VibeGuard’s minimum demonstrable prototype. This aligns with Phase 3B’s finding that ESP32- or STM32-class boards and commodity accelerometers are widely available and affordable for student teams.[^3][^10][^2][^6]

***

## 4. Embedded Platform Evaluation

### 4.1 Candidate Platforms

Phase 3B synthesis found that ESP32 and STM32-class microcontrollers appear as primary or viable platform choices across multiple concepts, including VibeGuard, thanks to their processing power, floating-point support, and peripheral richness. Other candidate platforms include Arduino-class ATmega boards, RP2040, and low-end STM32 variants; industrial discussions emphasize that embedded vibration analysis requires adequate CPU to perform FFTs or feature extraction at kHz sampling rates.[^2][^5][^4]

### 4.2 Comparative Factors

Key comparison axes include:

- Processing capability (clock speed, FPU availability) for time/frequency-domain processing.
- ADC or digital interface support (SPI/I²C) for sensors.
- Memory for buffering samples and computing features.
- Embedded ML support and libraries.
- Development ecosystem, debugging tools, and community support.
- Cost and board availability in India.

ESP32 offers dual-core tens-of-MHz to >200 MHz processing, integrated Wi-Fi/Bluetooth, and abundant RAM/flash, with strong Arduino/IDF ecosystems and good support for simple TinyML frameworks, making it well-suited for moderate-sophistication edge analytics. STM32 boards, particularly F4-series with FPU, similarly provide strong DSP capabilities and industrial-oriented peripherals but may involve steeper learning curves for students primarily experienced with Arduino-style development.[^3][^2][^5][^4]

Arduino-class ATmega boards have limited computational capacity and RAM, making real-time FFT or feature extraction at useful sampling rates more challenging, whereas RP2040 provides dual-core performance but slightly less mature vibration-analysis tooling.[^2]

### 4.3 Recommended Platform

**Primary architecture:** An ESP32 development board (e.g., widely available modules in India) is recommended as the main platform, combining adequate processing for time-domain statistics and basic FFT, simple sensor interfacing, strong community support, and reuse potential across other project concepts.[^3][^2]

**Alternative:** A mid-range STM32 (e.g., F4-series Nucleo board) is a viable alternative for teams preferring closer-to-industry tooling; however, for this CS/Cybersecurity-dominant student team, ESP32’s high-level development environment and familiarity offer an easier path to reliable implementation within the semester.

***

## 5. Signal Acquisition Architecture

### 5.1 Sampling Requirements

Industrial vibration condition monitoring typically aims for a frequency response 40–50 times the shaft RPM to capture relevant failure modes, implying sampling frequencies several kHz for typical lab motors. Honeywell’s condition monitoring guidelines indicate accelerometer ranges of 10 Hz–10 kHz for general rotating machinery, with key fault bands often below a few kHz.[^5][^4]

For VibeGuard’s minimum demonstrable prototype on small lab machines (e.g., fans, small motors), a sampling rate between 1 kHz and 5 kHz per axis is likely sufficient to detect changes in overall vibration magnitude and simple spectral patterns when an abnormal condition is deliberately introduced. Selecting a modest sampling rate reduces processing load and memory needs while preserving enough information for Normal/Abnormal discrimination.[^4][^5]

### 5.2 Anti-Aliasing and ADC Considerations

When using a digital MEMS accelerometer with an internal ADC and specified bandwidth, external anti-aliasing filters are generally unnecessary for a student-level prototype, provided the sensor is configured within its rated bandwidth and MCU-side sampling respects Nyquist criteria. For analog sensors, industrial practice recommends anti-aliasing filters before ADCs to prevent higher-frequency components from folding into the passband; this adds analog-design complexity that is avoidable with digital sensors.[^15][^6][^5][^4]

Given Phase 3B’s emphasis on keeping ADC choices simple and reserving high-resolution external ADCs for concepts such as TrueMoist, VibeGuard’s architecture will rely on the MEMS sensor’s internal ADC and digital interface rather than discrete external ADCs, minimizing hardware and debugging effort.[^2][^3]

### 5.3 Buffering and Timing Strategy

The MCU should acquire vibration data in fixed-length windows (e.g., 0.5–2 seconds per segment) using interrupt-driven sampling or sensor-driven data-ready signals, buffering data in RAM for feature extraction. For ESP32, a simple ring buffer per axis and timer/interrupt-based sampling or SPI burst reads provide sufficient timing stability; DMA is optional and can be reserved as an advanced optimization if needed.[^5]

***

## 6. Signal Processing Evaluation

### 6.1 Candidate Techniques

Condition monitoring literature and standards use a mix of time-domain and frequency-domain features, including RMS, peak, variance, standard deviation, crest factor, band-limited spectral energy, and FFT-based analysis. FFTs facilitate identification of dominant harmonics and sidebands associated with common faults such as imbalance or misalignment, while time-domain features often suffice for detecting gross changes in vibration magnitude.[^4][^5]

For a resource-constrained educational prototype, potential techniques include:

- Time-domain RMS and peak magnitude over windows.
- Standard deviation and variance as measures of overall vibration energy.
- Moving averages or exponential smoothing to track baseline.
- FFT or periodogram over modest window sizes (e.g., 256–1024 samples).
- Simple band-limited energy metrics (sum of spectral magnitudes in selected bands).

### 6.2 Comparative Assessment

RMS and peak measures are computationally cheap, robust, and widely used as overall vibration severity indicators tied to fatigue; they are easy to implement and interpret and align with ISO 10816’s velocity-based severity classes when converted appropriately. Standard deviation and variance similarly capture total vibration energy without requiring frequency-domain transforms, making them suitable for baseline vs abnormal discrimination.[^7][^5]

FFT-based frequency-domain analysis offers richer diagnostic insight but requires more computation and memory and careful windowing; still, for ESP32-level platforms, computing a modest FFT using integer or fixed-point libraries is feasible. Envelope analysis and more advanced spectral techniques are common in industry but unnecessary for minimum demonstrable success.[^5]

Given Phase 3B’s emphasis on keeping complexity appropriate and its identification of single-node isolation as the one genuinely hard open problem, VibeGuard’s prototype will adopt a primarily time-domain feature set (RMS, peak, standard deviation, possibly simple spectral energy) sufficient to detect deliberately introduced abnormal vibration on a controlled rig, with FFT as an optional extension if time permits.[^3][^2][^5]

***

## 7. Edge Intelligence Evaluation

### 7.1 Decision-Making Approaches

Common decision strategies for vibration monitoring include threshold-based classification (comparing features to calibrated limits), statistical anomaly detection (detecting deviations from baseline distributions), and lightweight machine-learning models such as decision trees or one-class anomaly detectors trained on normal data.[^4][^5]

Industrial practice often uses thresholds based on standards like ISO 10816 severity classes, with velocity or acceleration metrics categorized into ranges for machine classes. Academic work on edge anomaly detection explores simple clustering or one-class SVM on extracted features, but requires more training data and tuning.[^7][^5]

### 7.2 Comparative Assessment

Threshold-based classification is simple, explainable, and requires minimal data: the prototype can collect baseline segments under Normal operation, compute feature statistics, and set thresholds based on observed variance plus safety margins, then detect deliberate abnormal conditions when features exceed those thresholds. Statistical anomaly detection methods such as moving-mean and standard deviation bands offer similar simplicity and can be implemented without explicit training labels beyond Normal.[^5]

Lightweight TinyML approaches (small decision trees, one-class models) can add sophistication but increase implementation complexity, toolchain demands, and risk of overfitting given the limited dataset available in a student lab. Phase 3B noted that VibeGuard’s core engineering challenge is single-node noise isolation rather than model sophistication, so simpler decision logic aligned with the Engineering Design Review’s minimum success criteria is preferable.[^2][^3]

### 7.3 Recommended Strategy

The primary edge-intelligence strategy will be threshold-based classification on time-domain features, calibrated from Normal-operation measurements and validated against deliberately introduced Abnormal scenarios. A basic statistical anomaly detection layer (e.g., z-score or simple moving band rules) can be added to reduce false alarms and account for variability. TinyML (decision tree or simple anomaly detector trained via frameworks like TensorFlow Lite Micro) will be treated as an optional extension, developed only if core functionality is solid and remaining time permits.[^1][^2][^5]

***

## 8. Environmental Noise and False Alarm Analysis

### 8.1 Noise Sources

Honeywell and TE Connectivity highlight environmental and structural influences on vibration measurements, including nearby machine vibration, structural resonance, transient shocks, startup/shutdown behaviour, and mounting conditions. Mounting method (stud, adhesive, magnetic) significantly affects frequency response and sensor coupling, with poor mounting leading to distorted or attenuated signals.[^4][^5]

### 8.2 Single-Node Noise-Isolation Concept

Phase 3A and 3B identified VibeGuard’s defining innovation as single-node spatial/spectral noise isolation—distinguishing a target machine’s vibration signature from background or neighbouring machinery without an array of sensors. Industrial literature recognizes that accelerometers mounted on non-rotating parts can pick up vibration from multiple sources; spatial isolation is usually achieved with multiple sensors or careful placement.[^16][^2][^5]

For a student prototype, fully proving robust single-node isolation in a noisy industrial environment may be beyond scope, but the concept remains defensible and demonstrable in more controlled lab settings. The architecture can approximate noise isolation by:

- Mounting the sensor directly on the target machine with rigid coupling.
- Using controlled experiments where only the target machine’s operating condition changes while background remains fixed.
- Employing simple band-limited features if target-machine faults affect specific frequency bands more strongly than background.

### 8.3 False-Alarm Reduction Methods

Methods to reduce false alarms include:

- Averaging multiple windows before flagging an abnormal condition (persistence requirement).
- Ignoring transient spikes shorter than a defined duration.
- Using separate modes or thresholds for startup/shutdown vs steady-state.
- Ensuring consistent mounting and avoiding loose fixtures that introduce spurious vibration.

Given project constraints, VibeGuard will focus on demonstrating repeatable detection and classification in controlled scenarios and document environmental limitations, rather than claiming industrial-level false-alarm performance.[^3][^2][^5]

***

## 9. Mechanical Integration

### 9.1 Mounting Strategies

Condition monitoring guidelines emphasize that accelerometer mounting significantly affects measured vibration; stud or bolt mounting provides the best high-frequency coupling, while adhesive or magnetic mounts introduce resonance and may attenuate higher frequencies. For higher-frequency diagnostics, rigid mounting using threaded studs or firm adhesive is recommended.[^14][^5][^4]

For a student prototype on small machines, practical mounting options include:

- Bolt/stud mounting on a machined or drilled boss on the machine housing.
- High-strength adhesive on prepared flat surfaces.
- 3D-printed brackets firmly attached to the machine using screws.

### 9.2 Recommended Mechanical Integration

The preferred approach is to design a small 3D-printed bracket that:

- Holds the MEMS accelerometer PCB securely.
- Bolts to the machine housing using existing or student-drilled holes, providing rigid coupling.

This approach balances repeatability, ease of installation, and fabrication simplicity for the team, while providing adequate vibration transmission for the prototype’s frequency range. Adhesive-only mounting can be used for early lab tests but is less ideal for final demonstrations.[^5]

***

## 10. Electrical Architecture

### 10.1 Essential Interfaces and Circuits

An electrical architecture suitable for VibeGuard’s minimum demonstrable prototype includes:

- Sensor interface: SPI/I²C connection from ESP32 to the MEMS accelerometer, with proper supply decoupling.
- Power regulation: 5 V or 12 V input (e.g., from adapter), regulated down to 3.3 V for ESP32 and sensor using common regulator modules.
- Protection: Basic reverse-polarity protection and transient suppression (TVS) if necessary for lab safety.
- Status indicators: LEDs indicating power, Normal, Abnormal states; optional buzzer for alerts.
- Debug interface: USB/UART for firmware upload and logging.

Industrial sensors often use IEPE constant-current interfaces or 4–20 mA loops, but these are unnecessary for a student design using commodity MEMS modules. Signal conditioning beyond decoupling is minimal for digital sensors.[^9][^8]

### 10.2 Optional Display and Connectivity

An optional small OLED or LCD can show numeric feature values and current classification, enhancing educational value but not required for minimum success. Similarly, optional connectivity (Wi-Fi, Bluetooth) for streaming vibration data can be explored after core functionality is stable, but cloud processing is explicitly outside the minimum demonstrable scope.[^2]

***

## 11. Hardware Comparison and Selection

### 11.1 Candidate Sensor Modules

Available sensor modules suitable for student vibration projects include digital MEMS accelerometer boards based on chips designed for condition monitoring, as well as general-purpose accelerometers such as ADXL-type devices and more recent triaxial MEMS parts. Industrial MEMS vibration modules, like the 883M digital MEMS accelerometer or similar devices, are used in plant monitoring but may be more expensive; teaching labs often use cheaper general-purpose accelerometer breakout boards.[^10][^15][^6]

### 11.2 Candidate MCU Boards

ESP32 development boards are widely available at low cost, with integrated USB and programming support and strong community ecosystems; STM32-based Nucleo boards and Arduino-compatible boards are also common, but Phase 3B synthesis emphasized ESP32/STM32 as shared platform investments across concepts.[^3][^2]

### 11.3 Cost and Availability

Industrial catalogues show piezoelectric accelerometers and industrial vibration sensors at significantly higher price points than MEMS-based modules, reflecting their ruggedization and performance. By contrast, MEMS accelerometer breakout boards and ESP32 development boards are inexpensive and widely sold by electronics vendors and online retailers serving Indian markets, making it realistic to meet the ₹3000–₹5000 budget once sensor, MCU, power, indicators, and mechanical parts are included.[^13][^8][^9][^2][^3]

### 11.4 Recommended Hardware Set

The report therefore recommends:

- One digital triaxial MEMS accelerometer breakout board suitable for vibration monitoring.
- One ESP32 development board as the main MCU.
- Supporting components: power regulator module, LEDs, small buzzer (optional), wiring, connectors, and 3D-printed or simple fabricated bracket.

Fallback alternatives include using an STM32 Nucleo board if ESP32 is unavailable or a different MEMS accelerometer module of similar bandwidth if the preferred sensor cannot be sourced, provided the team validates its frequency response experimentally.

***

## 12. Cost and Manufacturability Assessment

### 12.1 Bill of Materials Considerations

Industrial piezoelectric accelerometers and velocity sensors demonstrate the wide range of possible costs depending on ruggedness and certifications; student-use MEMS sensors are orders of magnitude cheaper. Given typical prices for ESP32 boards and MEMS accelerometer modules in India, plus basic mechanical and electrical parts, a single-node VibeGuard prototype comfortably fits within the ₹3000–₹5000 budget envelope established in Phase 3B.[^8][^9][^2][^3][^4]

Assembly complexity is modest: soldering headers, connecting sensor breakout to MCU via jumper wires or simple PCBs, mounting the sensor bracket to the machine, and integrating indicators and power inputs. Fabrication requirements are limited to 3D printing or simple machining for brackets and basic hand tools.

### 12.2 Manufacturability and Reproducibility

Using commodity, off-the-shelf boards and sensors ensures reproducibility for future student teams, while avoiding specialized industrial components that may be difficult to source or overkill for educational prototypes. The architecture is designed so that one team can build and validate the prototype with standard lab equipment, and subsequent cohorts can replicate or iterate on the design.[^2][^3]

***

## 13. Engineering Trade-Off Analysis

### 13.1 Sensing Subsystem

- **Cost vs accuracy:** Piezoelectric accelerometers offer higher accuracy and bandwidth but at significantly higher cost and interface complexity; MEMS accelerometers sacrifice some performance but align with the budget and student capabilities. For VibeGuard’s educational goals, MEMS provides adequate fidelity.[^6][^4]
- **Complexity vs reliability:** Analog PE sensors require proper IEPE or 4–20 mA conditioning, adding complexity and potential failure points; digital MEMS modules simplify electronics and reduce wiring errors.[^9][^8]

### 13.2 Processing Subsystem

- **Computational load vs feature richness:** Time-domain RMS and simple statistics are cheap and robust; FFT and more advanced spectral features increase load but enable richer diagnostics. The selected pipeline prioritizes time-domain features with optional FFT, balancing reliability and learning opportunities.[^5]
- **Explainability vs sophistication:** Threshold-based logic is transparent and easy to debug; ML-based anomaly detection is more sophisticated but less explainable and likely unnecessary for the prototype’s minimum goals.[^3][^2]

### 13.3 Mechanical and Electrical Integration

- **Manufacturability vs performance:** Rigid stud/bracket mounting yields better vibration coupling but requires drilling or machining; adhesive mounting is simpler but may degrade high-frequency fidelity. The recommended bracket-based bolt mounting provides a good compromise within lab constraints.[^5]
- **Maintainability and scalability:** Using modular sensor and MCU boards makes maintenance and upgrades straightforward, and the architecture can scale to more sensors or features in future iterations without major redesign.

Overall, the selected architecture offers the best engineering balance for demonstrating VibeGuard’s hypothesis: it uses mature industrial principles (accelerometer-based vibration sensing, time-domain severity metrics) adapted to student-friendly hardware and complexity levels.

***

## 14. Prototype Implementation Strategy

### 14.1 Assembly Order

A practical build sequence that minimizes integration risk is:

1. **Power subsystem:** Assemble and test the power regulation and MCU board with indicators showing stable operation.
2. **Microcontroller bring-up:** Flash simple test firmware to confirm MCU boot, serial communication, and GPIO control.
3. **Sensor integration:** Connect the MEMS accelerometer breakout to the MCU and verify communication (e.g., reading static acceleration).
4. **Mechanical mounting:** Design and fabricate the bracket, then mount the sensor on the machine and verify stable readings.
5. **Raw vibration acquisition:** Implement streaming of sampled vibration data to serial or storage and validate basic signal quality during machine operation.
6. **Signal-processing features:** Add RMS, peak, and standard deviation computations over windows and verify numerical plausibility.
7. **Classification logic:** Implement threshold-based Normal/Abnormal decision and drive LEDs or buzzer accordingly.
8. **System refinement:** Tune thresholds, add optional FFT or anomaly-detection features, and refine mechanical mounting as needed.

### 14.2 Bring-Up Stages and Debugging

Each stage should have expected behaviour and success criteria (e.g., sensor outputs stable static acceleration when the machine is off; vibration features increase during abnormal conditions), with likely failure modes such as wiring errors, misconfigured sensor registers, or timing issues documented and addressed using serial logging and stepwise testing.[^5]

***

## 15. Prototype Validation Strategy and Test Scenarios

### 15.1 Validation Methodology

The prototype must demonstrate:

- A repeatable Normal operating condition with stable feature distributions.
- Deliberately introduced abnormal vibration (e.g., adding an imbalance or loosened fixture) producing statistically significant feature changes.
- Repeatable detection and classification of abnormal episodes as Abnormal.
- Acceptable false-alarm behaviour over multiple Normal runs.

Validation will use lab machines such as small motors, fans, or custom rigs, under controlled conditions that allow safe introduction of fault-like disturbances.

### 15.2 Example Scenarios

Realistic experimental scenarios include:

- **Balanced vs intentionally unbalanced rotating system:** Attach a small weight to a rotating disc to induce imbalance and observe increased vibration magnitude and possibly specific spectral peaks.[^5]
- **Loosened mechanical fixtures:** Gradually loosen a mounting bolt to increase mechanical play and vibration.
- **Induced resonance:** Vary machine speed to approach a resonance and observe increased vibration amplitude.
- **Repeatable mechanical disturbances:** Apply controlled taps or shocks to the machine housing to test transient behaviour and false-alarm handling.

Each scenario demonstrates aspects of the engineering hypothesis and is designed to be repeatable, safe, and educational.

***

## 16. Calibration and Debugging Strategy

### 16.1 Calibration Needs

Calibration is primarily needed for:

- Baseline Normal feature statistics (mean, variance) per machine and mounting configuration.
- Thresholds for classification, derived from baseline plus safety margins.

Industrial practice emphasizes calibration for accurate velocity or displacement measurements, but VibeGuard’s educational prototype focuses on relative changes and classification rather than absolute units. Simple, manual calibration procedures (collect Normal data, compute statistics) are sufficient and can be repeated if mounting or machine conditions change.[^7][^5]

### 16.2 Likely Engineering Issues and Debugging

Probable issues include poor sensor mounting leading to noisy or inconsistent readings, electrical noise from power supplies, unstable sampling due to timer misconfiguration, aliasing if sampling frequency is too low, and mis-set thresholds causing false positives/negatives. Debugging recommendations include inspecting raw time-series plots, checking sensor configuration registers, verifying sampling rates, and performing controlled baseline measurements to confirm feature stability before tuning thresholds.[^5]

***

## 17. Reliability and Team Implementation Assessment

### 17.1 Reliability Considerations

For lab-scale demonstrations, expected operating stability and repeatability are high if mounting and wiring are robust and code is tested under multiple scenarios; long-term drift and sensor durability issues are less critical but should be noted as limitations compared to industrial systems. Environmental sensitivity (temperature, nearby machines) should be documented as factors that may affect classification.[^4][^5]

### 17.2 Team Task Distribution

Given the team composition described in project documents—three stronger CS/Cybersecurity students, one beginner programmer, and two members focused on hardware and fabrication—the architecture supports the following allocation:[^2][^3]

- Experienced members: firmware design and implementation, signal-processing and classification algorithms, sensor integration, system-level debugging.
- Beginner programmer: tasks such as LED/display control, simple data logging, basic configuration modules, and contributing to testing scripts, under guidance.
- Other members: hardware sourcing, mechanical mounting design and fabrication, enclosure construction, assembly, documentation, and coordination with mechanical engineering labs.

This distribution maximizes project success and skill development while keeping critical embedded tasks with the most experienced students.

***

## 18. Procurement, Manufacturing, Risk and Readiness

### 18.1 Component Procurement and Manufacturing

Essential components (ESP32 board, MEMS accelerometer module, power supply, LEDs, wiring) are widely available from electronics suppliers and online marketplaces serving India. Optional components (display, buzzer, enclosures) can be procured as budget permits, and spare sensors or boards are advisable for risk mitigation.[^3][^2]

Manufacturing requirements include simple PCB or perfboard assembly, 3D-printed brackets or basic machined parts, and standard hand tools; no complex PCB fabrication or machining is mandatory.

### 18.2 Risk Assessment and Readiness

Major implementation risks include:

- Insufficient vibration coupling due to poor mounting.
- Single-node noise isolation proving weaker than desired in noisy environments.
- Overly aggressive or conservative thresholds causing misclassification.
- Schedule risk if extensive tuning or data collection is attempted late.

These risks are mitigated by early mechanical design and mounting tests, focusing validation on controlled lab rigs, prioritizing simple, explainable decision rules, and staging development so that basic sensing and classification are working well before optional features are attempted.[^2][^3][^5]

Phase 3B concluded that VibeGuard is highly feasible for the student team, with strong team-fit and budget alignment; the architecture defined here adheres to that feasibility picture and is ready to proceed into Phase 4 detailed design and prototype implementation.[^3][^2]

***

## 19. Phase 3C Memory Summary for VibeGuard

**Selected architecture:** Single-node vibration monitoring using a digital MEMS accelerometer rigidly mounted on a small lab machine, with local processing on an ESP32-class MCU performing time-domain feature extraction and threshold-based classification.[^6][^4][^2]

**Selected sensor:** Practical student choice is a triaxial MEMS accelerometer module with ~2–3 kHz usable bandwidth, SPI/I²C interface, and suitable resolution for condition monitoring; industrial piezoelectric accelerometers are acknowledged as stronger but not selected due to cost and complexity.[^6][^4]

**Selected MCU:** ESP32 development board as the primary platform, with STM32 as a viable alternative if needed.[^2]

**Processing approach:** Windowed sampling at 1–5 kHz, time-domain RMS, peak, standard deviation, and optional simple spectral energy or FFT for educational insight.[^5]

**Edge-intelligence strategy:** Threshold-based Normal/Abnormal classification calibrated from baseline Normal data, with optional statistical anomaly detection; TinyML considered an extension, not core.

**Electrical architecture:** Digital sensor interface via SPI/I²C, basic power regulation to 3.3 V, LEDs/buzzer for status, optional small display; minimal analog front-end due to digital sensor choice.[^8][^9]

**Mechanical integration:** Rigid bracket-mounted sensor on machine housing using bolts or screws; adhesive mounting used only for early experiments.[^5]

**Estimated cost:** Within ₹3000–₹5000 including MCU, sensor module, mechanical mounting, indicators, and miscellaneous parts, consistent with Phase 3B’s budget feasibility assessment.[^3][^2]

**Major engineering risks:** Single-node noise isolation robustness; mounting quality; threshold tuning; limited bandwidth of MEMS sensor relative to high-end PE sensors.[^4][^5]

**Unresolved questions:** Exact sensor model and its empirically validated frequency response; optimum sampling rate and window size; how well the single-node isolation concept holds in more complex vibration environments.

**Implementation priorities:** Early selection and characterization of the MEMS sensor; robust mechanical mounting; reliable acquisition and feature computation; simple, stable classification; progressive validation in controlled scenarios.

**Rationale:** The architecture uses mature industrial principles adapted to student-friendly hardware and complexity, validates the engineering hypothesis under lab conditions, fits the budget and team skills, and provides a strong foundation for future innovation in single-node isolation and edge intelligence.[^4][^2][^3]

# VibeGuard Architecture: A Low-Cost, DSP-First Framework for Reliable On-Device Vibration Anomaly Detection

## Architectural Investigation and Comparative Analysis

The objective of this investigation is to determine the most appropriate engineering architecture for the VibeGuard prototype, balancing technical validity, cost-effectiveness, and student-buildability within a strict budget and timeline. The analysis begins by evaluating candidate system-level approaches against the project's core constraints, including the directive to prioritize robustness to environmental noise and to treat classical Digital Signal Processing (DSP) as the primary baseline. The architectural choices are constrained by the need to operate on-device without cloud dependency, align with a mixed-skill student team, and remain within a ₹3000–₹5000 INR budget. The engineering philosophy prioritizes the validation of the core hypothesis—the ability to detect abnormal vibration locally—over achieving the sophistication of a complete industrial platform [[13](https://pmc.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/articles/PMC12609400/), [36](https://zbotic.in/iot-predictive-maintenance-vibration-sensors-on-esp32/?srsltid=AfmBOoqEDUylLB2FfE8n4KqcLjvznNgxrLurRXtJz9rtKcelcIZQfBru)].

Initial consideration was given to various sensing technologies, including piezoelectric vibration sensors and industrial transducers. However, these were quickly dismissed due to their significantly higher cost, which would consume a disproportionate portion of the project budget [[74](https://m.indiamart.com/proddetail/vibration-monitoring-sensor-2857197295388.html), [75](https://m.indiamart.com/proddetail/mems-accelerometer-3995166830.html)]. For instance, MEMS accelerometers designed for industrial vibration monitoring can cost upwards of ₹50,000, while USB-based units can reach ₹140,000, making them entirely unfeasible for a student project [[74](https://m.indiamart.com/proddetail/vibration-monitoring-sensor-2857197295388.html), [76](https://m.indiamart.com/proddetail/usb-accelerometer-sensor-21700550488.html)]. This constraint firmly establishes MEMS accelerometry as the only viable path forward. The subsequent analysis therefore focuses exclusively on single-sensor architectures based on low-cost MEMS accelerometers, as multi-sensor arrays introduce significant complexity in signal synchronization, data fusion, and mechanical mounting that exceeds the scope and resources of the project [[30](https://github.com/topics/vibration-sensor?l=c%2B%2B&o=desc&s=forks)]. The architecture will be purely edge-based, processing all data locally on a microcontroller to meet the requirement for standalone operation [[54](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/393360721_Development_of_a_Real-Time_Vibration_Monitoring_System_for_Drum_Tester_Machines_Using_ADXL345_Sensor_and_ESP32_Microcontroller)].

Within the domain of MEMS accelerometers, two primary candidates emerged from the literature for detailed comparative analysis: the ADXL345 and the MPU-6050 [[2](https://zbotic.in/accelerometer-buying-guide-india-adxl345-vs-mpu6050-vs-lis3dh/?srsltid=AfmBOornG9CGCSuPazWjSWuelG2QMkCN4izXB6jlDCg2-1z0n6f7mFR-), [56](https://zbotic.in/accelerometer-buying-guide-india-adxl345-vs-mpu6050-vs-lis3dh/?srsltid=AfmBOorurJOCEH4OIWeAsyOHq7_KwRyceLdd2p4W0e6Rg8usLu8SSOzD)]. The MPU-6050 is a 6-degree-of-freedom (6-DoF) sensor combining a 3-axis accelerometer and a 3-axis gyroscope, often marketed as a cost-effective IMU (Inertial Measurement Unit) [[95](https://www.tiktok.com/discover/accelerometer), [116](https://github.com/derdoktor667/ESP32_MPU6050)]. Its price point is highly attractive, frequently ranging from ₹80 to ₹200 in India, making it one of the cheapest options available [[4](https://robu.in/product/mpu-6050-gyro-sensor-2-accelerometer/), [73](https://www.ktron.in/product/adxl345-triple-axis-accelerometer-module/?srsltid=AfmBOoqS-gkWu5QpT7WV6eL2ORY-X2DSbfneFX_EeegxpiP4wnk0jncr)]. However, its inclusion of a gyroscope introduces a layer of complexity that may not be necessary and could even be detrimental for this specific application. Gyroscopes measure angular velocity and are susceptible to drift and temperature-related errors, which could complicate the interpretation of linear vibration data and introduce noise into the system [[193](https://www.analog.com/en/resources/analog-dialogue/raqs/raq-issue-144.html)]. One study noted that while the MPU6050 was recommended for stability in some mechanical vibration monitoring contexts, another found the ADXL345 to be superior for detecting faulty conditions, underscoring the nuanced nature of the trade-offs [[113](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/356622307_Performance_evaluation_of_accelerometers_ADXL345_and_MPU6050_exposed_to_random_vibrational_input), [146](https://www.mdpi.com/1424-8220/22/16/6015)].

The ADXL345, by contrast, is a dedicated 3-axis digital accelerometer. While slightly more expensive, typically priced between ₹150 and ₹350, its focused functionality offers distinct advantages for this project [[2](https://zbotic.in/accelerometer-buying-guide-india-adxl345-vs-mpu6050-vs-lis3dh/?srsltid=AfmBOornG9CGCSuPazWjSWuelG2QMkCN4izXB6jlDCg2-1z0n6f7mFR-), [23](https://www.mathaelectronics.com/product/adxl345/)]. As a simpler device, it is less susceptible to certain types of electronic interference and has fewer sources of potential error compared to the more complex MPU-6050 [[57](https://forum.arduino.cc/t/adxl345-vs-mpu6050-vs-bno055/1218136)]. It features a high resolution of 13-bit measurements across selectable ranges from ±2g to ±16g, providing sufficient sensitivity for detecting the target anomalies [[26](https://www.tradeindia.com/products/adxl345-sensor-module-4416569.html), [29](https://techdelivers.com/adxl345-triple-axis-accelerometer-board?tag=sensor&srsltid=AfmBOopOAnbIZj2VyseKSpVodzqkKxqle4dtjtjITKYufNsDOETCDTSq)]. Crucially, its widespread use in academic and hobbyist projects means there is a vast body of documentation, tutorials, and pre-existing libraries for interfacing with platforms like Arduino and ESP32, which significantly de-risks the implementation for a student team [[38](https://www.bettlink.com/blog/adxl345-guide), [39](https://pcbsync.com/adxl345-arduino/), [40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=XSzRwJNJ4u8)]. Numerous published works have successfully employed the ADXL345 for vibration analysis, including real-time motor health monitoring and drum tester machine monitoring, confirming its suitability and reliability for such tasks [[54](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/393360721_Development_of_a_Real-Time_Vibration_Monitoring_System_for_Drum_Tester_Machines_Using_ADXL345_Sensor_and_ESP32_Microcontroller), [55](https://www.ijprems.com/ijprems-paper/vibration-based-motor-health-monitoring-system-using-esp32-and-adxl345), [93](https://in.linkedin.com/in/abhranil-sarkar)]. Given the project's explicit goal of distinguishing normal vibration from deliberate abnormalities like imbalance or misalignment, the cleaner, more direct measurement provided by the ADXL345's accelerometer array makes it the more prudent and less risky choice. The decision favors a component whose behavior is well-understood and whose ecosystem supports rapid development over one that offers extra features at the cost of increased complexity and potential ambiguity.

| Feature | ADXL345 | MPU-6050 |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Sensor Type** | 3-Axis Digital Accelerometer [[114](https://www.sparkfun.com/datasheets/Sensors/Accelerometer/ADXL345.pdf?srsltid=AfmBOoqhS2gEB2Y-FDs6efYQMjpPhJ_E39w16rAO5J5MF0F6mDKxx-mj)] | 3-Axis Accelerometer + 3-Axis Gyroscope (6-DoF) [[95](https://www.tiktok.com/discover/accelerometer)] |
| **Price in India** | ₹150 – ₹350 [[2](https://zbotic.in/accelerometer-buying-guide-india-adxl345-vs-mpu6050-vs-lis3dh/?srsltid=AfmBOornG9CGCSuPazWjSWuelG2QMkCN4izXB6jlDCg2-1z0n6f7mFR-), [56](https://zbotic.in/accelerometer-buying-guide-india-adxl345-vs-mpu6050-vs-lis3dh/?srsltid=AfmBOorurJOCEH4OIWeAsyOHq7_KwRyceLdd2p4W0e6Rg8usLu8SSOzD)] | ₹80 – ₹200 [[2](https://zbotic.in/accelerometer-buying-guide-india-adxl345-vs-mpu6050-vs-lis3dh/?srsltid=AfmBOornG9CGCSuPazWjSWuelG2QMkCN4izXB6jlDCg2-1z0n6f7mFR-), [56](https://zbotic.in/accelerometer-buying-guide-india-adxl345-vs-mpu6050-vs-lis3dh/?srsltid=AfmBOorurJOCEH4OIWeAsyOHq7_KwRyceLdd2p4W0e6Rg8usLu8SSOzD)] |
| **Interface** | I2C, SPI [[23](https://www.mathaelectronics.com/product/adxl345/)] | I2C [[116](https://github.com/derdoktor667/ESP32_MPU6050)] |
| **Key Advantage** | Simplicity, high resolution (13-bit), proven performance in academic projects [[26](https://www.tradeindia.com/products/adxl345-sensor-module-4416569.html), [38](https://www.bettlink.com/blog/adxl345-guide)] | Lower cost, integrated motion tracking [[95](https://www.tiktok.com/discover/accelerometer)] |
| **Key Disadvantage** | Slightly higher cost than MPU-6050 [[2](https://zbotic.in/accelerometer-buying-guide-india-adxl345-vs-mpu6050-vs-lis3dh/?srsltid=AfmBOornG9CGCSuPazWjSWuelG2QMkCN4izXB6jlDCg2-1z0n6f7mFR-)] | Potential for gyroscopic cross-talk and drift; unnecessary complexity for vibration-only task [[57](https://forum.arduino.cc/t/adxl345-vs-mpu6050-vs-bno055/1218136), [193](https://www.analog.com/en/resources/analog-dialogue/raqs/raq-issue-144.html)] |
| **Suitability for VibeGuard** | High: Direct measurement of linear acceleration with low implementation risk [[54](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/393360721_Development_of_a_Real-Time_Vibration_Monitoring_System_for_Drum_Tester_Machines_Using_ADXL345_Sensor_and_ESP32_Microcontroller), [55](https://www.ijprems.com/ijprems-paper/vibration-based-motor-health-monitoring-system-using-esp32-and-adxl345)] | Medium: Over-specification introduces complexity that does not add value to the core hypothesis [[57](https://forum.arduino.cc/t/adxl345-vs-mpu6050-vs-bno055/1218136)] |

Based on this analysis, the selected architecture will be a single-node system centered around the ADXL345 MEMS accelerometer. This choice directly addresses the need for a robust, cost-effective, and implementable solution. The focus remains on leveraging the sensor's ability to accurately capture linear vibration data, which is the core requirement for validating the engineering hypothesis. The architecture will not incorporate advanced features like gyroscopic data fusion or complex multi-modal sensing, as these are outside the project's defined scope and budget. The emphasis will instead be on creating a reliable physical installation and developing a simple yet effective signal processing pipeline to interpret the accelerometer's output.

## Sensor Technology Evaluation and Selection

The selection of the vibration sensor is arguably the most critical decision in the VibeGuard architecture, as it forms the foundation of the entire measurement system. The chosen technology must be capable of capturing the target mechanical phenomena—namely, lower-frequency anomalies such as imbalance and misalignment—while remaining compatible with the project's stringent budget of ₹3000–₹5000 and the skill level of a student team. The investigation confirms that industrial-grade piezoelectric accelerometers or specialized high-performance MEMS sensors are financially inaccessible, with prices starting at tens of thousands of rupees [[74](https://m.indiamart.com/proddetail/vibration-monitoring-sensor-2857197295388.html), [75](https://m.indiamart.com/proddetail/mems-accelerometer-3995166830.html)]. Therefore, the analysis is confined to the class of low-cost MEMS accelerometers readily available through Indian suppliers.

Two primary candidates consistently appear in the provided materials for educational and low-cost IoT projects: the ADXL345 and the MPU-6050 [[2](https://zbotic.in/accelerometer-buying-guide-india-adxl345-vs-mpu6050-vs-lis3dh/?srsltid=AfmBOornG9CGCSuPazWjSWuelG2QMkCN4izXB6jlDCg2-1z0n6f7mFR-), [56](https://zbotic.in/accelerometer-buying-guide-india-adxl345-vs-mpu6050-vs-lis3dh/?srsltid=AfmBOorurJOCEH4OIWeAsyOHq7_KwRyceLdd2p4W0e6Rg8usLu8SSOzD), [152](https://zbotic.in/accelerometer-buying-guide-india-adxl345-vs-mpu6050-vs-lis3dh/?srsltid=AfmBOoq0TWhef4pbYfpD7vvFdVrnWSZLzOjtf64Z-Aa5UbwNDR7LLuKc)]. The MPU-6050 is a popular choice among hobbyists due to its extremely low cost, often available for as little as ₹80-₹200, and its integration of both an accelerometer and a gyroscope onto a single chip [[4](https://robu.in/product/mpu-6050-gyro-sensor-2-accelerometer/), [73](https://www.ktron.in/product/adxl345-triple-axis-accelerometer-module/?srsltid=AfmBOoqS-gkWu5QpT7WV6eL2ORY-X2DSbfneFX_EeegxpiP4wnk0jncr)]. While this combination is powerful for applications like drone stabilization or gesture recognition, it introduces elements that are not directly relevant to the core task of linear vibration analysis [[95](https://www.tiktok.com/discover/accelerometer)]. The gyroscope measures angular velocity, which is prone to issues like bias drift and temperature sensitivity, potentially complicating the signal processing chain and introducing unwanted artifacts when analyzing linear acceleration data [[193](https://www.analog.com/en/resources/analog-dialogue/raqs/raq-issue-144.html)]. Furthermore, studies comparing the two devices for vibration monitoring have produced mixed results; one paper recommended the MPU6050 for its stability, while another concluded the ADXL345 was better at detecting faulty conditions, indicating that the "better" sensor can depend heavily on the specific application and implementation [[113](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/356622307_Performance_evaluation_of_accelerometers_ADXL345_and_MPU6050_exposed_to_random_vibrational_input), [146](https://www.mdpi.com/1424-8220/22/16/6015)].

The ADXL345, while generally priced slightly higher (typically ₹150–₹350), is a dedicated 3-axis digital accelerometer [[2](https://zbotic.in/accelerometer-buying-guide-india-adxl345-vs-mpu6050-vs-lis3dh/?srsltid=AfmBOornG9CGCSuPazWjSWuelG2QMkCN4izXB6jlDCg2-1z0n6f7mFR-), [23](https://www.mathaelectronics.com/product/adxl345/)]. Its specialization for measuring linear acceleration makes it a more straightforward and direct tool for the VibeGuard's purpose. It boasts a high-resolution 13-bit measurement capability and allows for selectable full-scale ranges from ±2g to ±16g, offering flexibility for different vibration amplitudes [[26](https://www.tradeindia.com/products/adxl345-sensor-module-4416569.html), [29](https://techdelivers.com/adxl345-triple-axis-accelerometer-board?tag=sensor&srsltid=AfmBOopOAnbIZj2VyseKSpVodzqkKxqle4dtjtjITKYufNsDOETCDTSq)]. Critically, the ADXL345 benefits from extensive community support and a wealth of existing projects, with numerous guides detailing its use with microcontrollers like the ESP32 and Arduino [[38](https://www.bettlink.com/blog/adxl345-guide), [39](https://pcbsync.com/adxl345-arduino/), [40](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=XSzRwJNJ4u8)]. This rich ecosystem of tutorials, code examples, and troubleshooting advice significantly lowers the barrier to entry for students and reduces the risk of implementation failure. Multiple research papers and student projects have successfully used the ADXL345 for real-time vibration monitoring and fault detection, demonstrating its proven efficacy in similar contexts [[54](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/393360721_Development_of_a_Real-Time_Vibration_Monitoring_System_for_Drum_Tester_Machines_Using_ADXL345_Sensor_and_ESP32_Microcontroller), [55](https://www.ijprems.com/ijprems-paper/vibration-based-motor-health-monitoring-system-using-esp32-and-adxl345), [93](https://in.linkedin.com/in/abhranil-sarkar)].

Regarding frequency response, the user's directive specifies a focus on lower-frequency anomalies like imbalance and misalignment, rather than high-frequency bearing faults [[36](https://zbotic.in/iot-predictive-maintenance-vibration-sensors-on-esp32/?srsltid=AfmBOoqEDUylLB2FfE8n4KqcLjvznNgxrLurRXtJz9rtKcelcIZQfBru)]. The ADXL345 datasheet indicates an output data rate up to 3200Hz, theoretically allowing it to capture frequencies up to 1600Hz [[28](https://www.electronicscomp.com/adxl345-triple-axis-linear-accelerometer), [187](https://forums.adafruit.com/viewtopic.php?t=183954)]. While this bandwidth is more than sufficient for the targeted faults, it also ensures that the architecture is scalable. If future iterations of the project wish to explore more subtle anomalies, the sensor is technically capable of doing so. The project's scope explicitly avoids the need for the ultra-low noise densities (e.g., <100 µg/√Hz) characteristic of high-end industrial sensors, making the ADXL345's typical noise density of around 290 µg/√Hz acceptable for this educational prototype [[115](https://www.scribd.com/document/344880140/ADXL345)]. The primary engineering challenge identified in the literature for any MEMS-based system is not raw sensor performance but rather the management of environmental noise and ensuring proper mechanical coupling, challenges that are addressed through architectural design rather than sensor selection alone [[32](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s43926-025-00142-4), [79](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/iel8/6287639/6514899/10752530.pdf)].

Ultimately, the decision rests on a balance of cost, simplicity, and proven reliability. The minor additional cost of the ADXL345 is justified by its functional purity, reduced implementation risk, and the extensive support network surrounding it. These factors align perfectly with the project's goals of being an educational, reproducible, and reliable prototype. The sensor's specifications are more than adequate for detecting the deliberately introduced abnormal conditions of interest. Therefore, the **ADXL345** is the unequivocally recommended sensing technology for the VibeGuard architecture. Its selection represents the optimal compromise between performance requirements, budgetary constraints, and the practical realities of student-led development.

## Embedded Platform and Electrical Architecture

The selection of the embedded processing platform is a pivotal decision that dictates the system's computational capabilities, memory resources, peripheral support, power consumption, and overall development environment. For the VibeGuard project, the ideal platform must offer a compelling balance of low cost, sufficient performance for the required signal processing tasks, and ease of use for a student team with mixed expertise. The analysis of candidate platforms—primarily the ESP32 family, STM32 series, and Arduino-class MCUs—converges on the ESP32 as the most suitable choice.

The ESP32 microcontroller stands out due to its exceptional combination of features and affordability. Development boards featuring the ESP32 are widely available in India for under ₹500, with prices often cited around ₹350–₹554 [[78](https://zbotic.in/product/esp32-programmer-board/?srsltid=AfmBOoq4ml9135awfnwdOswCOdeO5cNO1P1ReNbnsn2xPUcf2vy9NTtQ), [150](https://aiiottech.in/product-category/development-boards/)]. This low cost fits comfortably within the project's tight budget. More importantly, the ESP32 integrates a dual-core processor, substantial RAM, and built-in Wi-Fi and Bluetooth connectivity [[90](https://blog.huebits.in/top-10-trending-industrial-iot-projects-for-2025-with-source-code/), [207](https://www.electropi.in/esp32-development-board-with-wifi-bluetooth-india)]. The dual-core architecture can be leveraged to run real-time tasks, such as continuous data acquisition, on one core while the other core handles more computationally intensive tasks like FFT analysis or managing communication protocols. The integrated wireless capabilities are a significant advantage, providing a clear pathway for future scalability beyond the minimum demonstrable prototype, which explicitly requires a standalone device [[9](https://esp32.com/viewtopic.php?t=5038)]. In contrast, platforms like the STM32, while powerful, often come at a higher price point (with basic boards costing around ₹1530 [[151](https://hubtronics.in/microcontroller-development-boards?page=3&srsltid=AfmBOooZpNMxEea-YdxjbNMA9d8pexK1fvoxUiD_RMKJszaAuQzm4aGj)]) and may require a more complex toolchain for firmware development, presenting a steeper learning curve for beginners [[206](https://probots.co.in/diy-electronic-modules/development-boards-single-board-computers-raspberry-pi/stm32-development-board.html)]. Similarly, classic Arduino boards lack native wireless, and while the RP2040 (used in the Pico) is very low-cost (~₹200-265 [[204](https://hubtronics.in/rp2040-zero-mcu-board?srsltid=AfmBOoqlx7blx-_CIZV32FBBDUx44akt9O4ucqbbKizL7XkqBc8SJwqD), [205](https://www.rees52.in/raspberry-pi-pico-microcontroller-development-board-with-versatile-board-built-using-rp2040-chip-8668555.html)]), adding wireless functionality would necessitate purchasing and integrating a separate module, increasing both cost and complexity [[149](https://hubtronics.in/ultimate-pico-rp2040-dev-board?srsltid=AfmBOoqxElPmX3xQWdmwkfYtMK02ddMqd2bz1hgtlYjtXq9UPk9Q9xva), [176](https://makerbazar.in/products/arduino-nano-rp2040-connect-abx00052?srsltid=AfmBOopzxzVvDimkd0IquB8JHEHHBqnirRF-0G7eJTmqHRxAX5sqlH8z)].

The ESP32's strength is further amplified by its mature software ecosystem. It is fully supported by the Arduino IDE, which hosts a library repository of over 9,000 entries [[118](https://www.arduinolibraries.info/libraries)]. This vast collection includes optimized libraries for communicating with the ADXL345 sensor (e.g., Adafruit's driver) and for performing Fast Fourier Transforms (e.g., KISS FFT) [[10](https://blog.adafruit.com/2026/04/28/the-kiss-fft-arduino-esp32-library/), [38](https://www.bettlink.com/blog/adxl345-guide)]. The availability of these pre-built, tested components dramatically accelerates development and minimizes debugging time for the student team. The extensive online community provides a wealth of tutorials, forums, and example code, which is invaluable for troubleshooting common issues related to sensor interfacing, wireless communication, or real-time programming [[9](https://esp32.com/viewtopic.php?t=5038), [88](https://www.facebook.com/groups/1006568440013226/posts/1808533646483364/)]. This accessible environment is perfectly suited to the team's composition, where three members have stronger CS backgrounds and can leverage this ecosystem effectively, while the beginner programmer can work on more manageable firmware tasks using familiar frameworks.

The electrical architecture for the VibeGuard prototype can be kept intentionally simple. Since the selected ADXL345 sensor is a digital device, it communicates with the ESP32 via a standard I2C interface [[114](https://www.sparkfun.com/datasheets/Sensors/Accelerometer/ADXL345.pdf?srsltid=AfmBOoqhS2gEB2Y-FDs6efYQMjpPhJ_E39w16rAO5J5MF0F6mDKxx-mj)]. This requires only four connections: VCC (power), GND (ground), SCL (clock), and SDA (data). Pull-up resistors on the SDA and SCL lines are necessary for proper I2C bus operation, but these are simple passive components. Power regulation is also straightforward; the ESP32 can be powered via USB or a DC-DC converter, and the ADXL345 can operate from either 3.3V or 5V logic levels, which is easily managed by the ESP32's onboard voltage regulator or external circuitry [[5](https://robu.in/product/adxl345-tripple-axis-accelerometer-board/), [23](https://www.mathaelectronics.com/product/adxl345/)]. There is no need for a complex analog front-end or signal conditioning circuit, as the sensor performs the analog-to-digital conversion internally. Status indicators, such as LEDs connected to GPIO pins on the ESP32, can provide visual feedback on the system's state (e.g., "Normal," "Abnormal," "Acquiring Data"), fulfilling part of the minimum demonstrable success criteria in a low-cost manner. The overall electrical design prioritizes reliability and ease of assembly, avoiding unnecessary components to stay within budget and reduce potential points of failure. This minimalist approach ensures that the focus remains on the core signal processing and classification algorithms.

## Signal Processing and Edge Intelligence Strategy

The strategy for processing vibration data and making intelligent decisions on the edge is central to the VibeGuard's engineering hypothesis. The user's directive is clear: classical Digital Signal Processing (DSP) must serve as the baseline due to its maturity, explainability, and low implementation risk. Advanced techniques like TinyML are to be considered only as a potential enhancement if they provide a clear and justifiable benefit that outweighs their increased complexity. This philosophy steers the architecture toward a pragmatic, layered approach that maximizes reliability and student feasibility.

For the signal processing pipeline, a hybrid, tiered methodology is recommended. The first and most fundamental layer involves time-domain analysis. The simplest and most robust feature to calculate is the Root Mean Square (RMS) value of the acceleration signal [[99](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/iel7/6287639/9668973/09913462.pdf)]. The RMS value is a direct measure of the overall energy or magnitude of the vibration and is highly effective at detecting gross changes indicative of large, persistent faults like rotor imbalance or structural looseness [[147](https://www.arduinolibraries.info/libraries/mpu6050_vibration-rms), [188](https://www.researchgate.net/figure/The-vibration-data-recorded-using-ADXL345-accelerometer_tbl4_362647463)]. Calculating the RMS value is computationally inexpensive and can be performed continuously on the ESP32, providing a constant stream of data representing the machine's vibrational energy. Alongside RMS, calculating the standard deviation or variance of the signal can also provide useful information about the signal's fluctuation and help identify periods of instability [[99](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/iel7/6287639/9668973/09913462.pdf)].

To move beyond simple energy detection and begin identifying specific fault signatures, a second layer involving frequency-domain analysis is necessary. The Fast Fourier Transform (FFT) is the canonical tool for converting a time-domain signal into its constituent frequencies [[10](https://blog.adafruit.com/2026/04/28/the-kiss-fft-arduino-esp32-library/)]. By applying an FFT to a window of acquired data, the system can generate a frequency spectrum that reveals dominant peaks corresponding to the machine's rotational speed and its harmonics. For instance, a significant peak at the first harmonic of the rotational frequency is a classic indicator of mass imbalance [[99](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/iel7/6287639/9668973/09913462.pdf)]. The availability of efficient C-based FFT libraries like KISS FFT, which are designed to integrate seamlessly with the Arduino framework on the ESP32, makes this implementation feasible [[10](https://blog.adafruit.com/2026/04/28/the-kiss-fft-arduino-esp32-library/), [11](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=Mgh2WblO5_c)]. However, performing a full FFT on every sample is computationally demanding and would consume too much of the ESP32's processing resources. A more practical approach is to perform the FFT intermittently. The system can rely on the fast RMS calculation for real-time monitoring. Only when the RMS value crosses a predefined threshold—an initial indicator of an anomaly—would the system trigger a more resource-intensive process: acquiring a larger buffer of data and executing an FFT on that buffer to characterize the nature of the fault.

This leads to the edge intelligence strategy. For the minimum demonstrable prototype, the strongest and most reliable approach is a **threshold-based classifier**. This method aligns perfectly with the project's goals and constraints. During a calibration or "normal condition" phase, the system would continuously monitor the RMS value and establish a baseline. A threshold, perhaps set at a multiple of the standard deviation above the mean normal RMS, would then be defined. Any subsequent RMS reading that exceeds this threshold would be classified as "Abnormal." This approach is deterministic, has near-zero inference latency, requires minimal code, and is completely transparent and explainable—a key advantage over "black box" ML models. It directly satisfies the minimum demonstrable success criteria with maximum engineering integrity.

TinyML, while a powerful emerging technology, introduces a significant increase in implementation risk and complexity that is not warranted for this initial prototype. Research demonstrates that TinyML models can achieve very high accuracy in motor fault detection, sometimes exceeding 96% [[32](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s43926-025-00142-4), [33](https://arxiv.org/html/2508.16553v1)]. However, realizing this performance requires a non-trivial effort: collecting and meticulously labeling a large dataset of vibration signals under both normal and abnormal operating conditions [[180](https://blog.endaq.com/using-vibration-data-for-high-level-machine-learning)]. It also demands expertise in machine learning frameworks, model training, and optimization techniques like quantization to make the model small and fast enough to run on an MCU [[33](https://arxiv.org/html/2508.16553v1)]. Furthermore, studies highlight that even sophisticated ML models can be sensitive to environmental noise, potentially leading to false positives if not carefully designed, a problem the project aims to solve through architectural robustness [[32](https://link.springer.com/article/10.1007/s43926-025-00142-4)]. Therefore, while the potential for a future upgrade to a TinyML-based classifier exists, it should be treated as a secondary enhancement. The primary focus for the VibeGuard prototype must remain on building and validating the robust, reliable, and interpretable baseline system using classical DSP and a simple threshold classifier.

## Mechanical Integration and Environmental Noise Mitigation

The mechanical integration of the sensor and the mitigation of environmental noise represent the most significant engineering risks for the VibeGuard prototype. A flawed physical installation can render even the most sophisticated electronics ineffective, as poor vibration coupling will result in a corrupted signal that cannot be corrected by software. The user's directive to prioritize robustness to environmental interference underscores the paramount importance of this subsystem. The architecture must be designed to ensure consistent, repeatable measurements while filtering out extraneous noise from nearby machinery and ambient sources.

The sensor mounting strategy is the first line of defense for ensuring signal fidelity. The choice of mount directly impacts the efficiency of vibration transfer from the machine frame to the sensor die. Common methods include adhesive mounting, magnetic mounting, and rigid bolt mounting. Magnetic mounts offer convenience and repositionability but suffer from poor repeatability and can easily become detached during testing, leading to inconsistent data [[103](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/iel8/7361/4427201/11080221.pdf)]. Adhesive mounting, such as with epoxy or industrial-strength glue, provides a more secure bond but can be difficult to remove and may degrade over time. For a prototype intended for repeated experiments and demonstrations, the most robust and reliable method is **rigid bolt mounting**. Securing the sensor package directly to a clean, flat surface on the machine housing with a small screw or bolt ensures maximum and consistent vibration coupling [[103](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/iel8/7361/4427201/11080221.pdf)]. This method, while requiring more setup time, guarantees the highest possible fidelity of the measured signal and is essential for achieving repeatable results. The design of a custom printed enclosure can facilitate this by incorporating a tapped hole or a bracket for secure sensor attachment [[86](https://www.mdpi.com/1424-8220/23/24/9677)].

Once the sensor is physically secured, the architecture must address the challenge of discriminating the target machine's vibration from ambient noise. The project's core hypothesis depends on this ability. Several architectural principles are employed to enhance this discrimination. First, **proximity** is key. Placing the sensor as close as physically possible to the source of the vibration naturally increases the signal-to-noise ratio, as the target signal attenuates less over distance than many ambient noise sources. Second, the **feature extraction strategy** plays a crucial role. By focusing on the Root Mean Square (RMS) value for baseline monitoring, the system prioritizes overall vibrational energy, a property inherent to the machine itself [[99](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/iel7/6287639/9668973/09913462.pdf)]. When frequency-domain analysis is triggered, the ability to identify characteristic fault frequencies (e.g., rotational harmonics) provides another layer of discrimination. Machine-specific faults often produce distinct spectral signatures that may occupy different frequency bands than general ambient factory noise, which can be broadband or concentrated in other octaves [[21](https://www.science.gov/topicpages/t/three-component+digital+seismometers), [59](https://thermairsystems.com/wp-content/uploads/2011/10/ASHRAE-HANDBOOK-Sound-and-Vibration-Control.pdf)]. HVAC systems, for example, often generate significant noise in octave bands from 250 Hz to 8000 Hz [[59](https://thermairsystems.com/wp-content/uploads/2011/10/ASHRAE-HANDBOOK-Sound-and-Vibration-Control.pdf)], whereas a misaligned shaft might produce a strong 1x rotational frequency peak at a much lower frequency. Filtering the signal in these known bands can help isolate the machine's own vibrations.

Finally, the **in-situ baseline establishment** is a critical feature of the proposed architecture. Instead of relying on a generic threshold, the system will measure and store the "Normal" condition parameters (RMS value and spectral signature) while the machine is running under controlled, known-good conditions. This process inherently captures the local background noise level at that specific moment and location. Consequently, the system becomes implicitly calibrated to its immediate environment, improving its ability to detect deviations caused by the deliberately introduced abnormality. This approach acknowledges that absolute vibration levels are meaningless without context; the relative change from a verified normal state is what truly matters. The architecture deliberately avoids proposing complex, multi-node spatial filtering arrays, as their implementation complexity and cost are prohibitive. Instead, it relies on the quality of the single sensor's measurement, the robustness of the physical mounting, and the intelligence of the classification logic to achieve the desired outcome. This simplification is a conscious trade-off to manage risk and ensure the project remains achievable within the specified constraints.

## Final Architecture Recommendation and Implementation Feasibility

This report has systematically investigated and analyzed the engineering architecture for the VibeGuard predictive maintenance prototype, culminating in a definitive recommendation that prioritizes engineering validity, cost-effectiveness, and student-buildability. The final architecture is designed to robustly validate the project's core engineering hypothesis within the strict constraints of a ₹3000–₹5000 budget, a semester-long timeline, and a five-member student team. Every component and strategy has been selected based on evidence from the provided sources, adhering to the principle of choosing the simplest solution that meets the requirements.

The recommended engineering architecture for VibeGuard is as follows:

*   **Sensing Architecture:** A single-axis or triaxial **ADXL345** MEMS accelerometer. This sensor is the optimal choice due to its excellent balance of cost (₹150–₹350), proven reliability in academic vibration monitoring projects, high resolution (13-bit), and implementation simplicity [[2](https://zbotic.in/accelerometer-buying-guide-india-adxl345-vs-mpu6050-vs-lis3dh/?srsltid=AfmBOornG9CGCSuPazWjSWuelG2QMkCN4izXB6jlDCg2-1z0n6f7mFR-), [38](https://www.bettlink.com/blog/adxl345-guide), [54](https://www.researchgate.net/publication/393360721_Development_of_a_Real-Time_Vibration_Monitoring_System_for_Drum_Tester_Machines_Using_ADXL345_Sensor_and_ESP32_Microcontroller)].
*   **Processing Architecture:** An **ESP32** microcontroller development board. This platform provides the best combination of affordability (under ₹500), sufficient dual-core processing power, integrated Wi-Fi/Bluetooth for future scalability, and a vast, supportive software ecosystem with ready-made libraries for sensor interfacing and signal processing [[118](https://www.arduinolibraries.info/libraries), [150](https://aiiottech.in/product-category/development-boards/), [207](https://www.electropi.in/esp32-development-board-with-wifi-bluetooth-india)].
*   **Signal Acquisition:** Continuous sampling of the ADXL345's digital output via the I2C interface. The output data rate can be configured up to 3200 Hz, though a lower rate (e.g., >100 Hz) will suffice for detecting the target lower-frequency faults [[28](https://www.electronicscomp.com/adxl345-triple-axis-linear-accelerometer), [187](https://forums.adafruit.com/viewtopic.php?t=183954)].
*   **Signal Processing Pipeline:** A hybrid, tiered approach. The system will continuously calculate the **Root Mean Square (RMS)** value of the vibration signal in the time domain. This provides a low-latency, computationally cheap measure of overall vibrational energy. Upon detection of an anomaly via the RMS threshold, the system will acquire a larger buffer of data and perform a **Fast Fourier Transform (FFT)** to analyze the frequency content and identify characteristic fault signatures [[10](https://blog.adafruit.com/2026/04/28/the-kiss-fft-arduino-esp32-library/), [99](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/iel7/6287639/9668973/09913462.pdf)].
*   **Edge Intelligence Strategy:** A **threshold-based classifier**. The system will establish a "Normal" operating envelope during an initial calibration phase. Any subsequent measurement that deviates significantly from this baseline (specifically, an RMS value exceeding a calculated threshold) will be classified as "Abnormal." This method is deterministic, has zero inference latency, and is fully transparent, making it ideal for the minimum demonstrable prototype [[96](https://dl.acm.org/doi/fullHtml/10.1145/3568671), [99](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/iel7/6287639/9668973/09913462.pdf)].
*   **Electrical Architecture:** A minimalist design consisting of the ESP32 board, the ADXL345 sensor, basic pull-up resistors for the I2C bus, and a simple power supply (e.g., USB or a 3.7V Li-ion battery with a voltage regulator). No complex analog front-end is required due to the sensor's digital nature [[114](https://www.sparkfun.com/datasheets/Sensors/Accelerometer/ADXL345.pdf?srsltid=AfmBOoqhS2gEB2Y-FDs6efYQMjpPhJ_E39w16rAO5J5MF0F6mDKxx-mj)].
*   **Mechanical Integration:** The sensor will be **rigidly bolt-mounted** to a clean, flat surface on the machine frame. This is the most critical step for ensuring accurate and repeatable vibration transfer, thereby maximizing signal fidelity and minimizing the impact of loose connections or poor coupling [[103](https://ieeexplore.ieee.org/iel8/7361/4427201/11080221.pdf)].

The estimated Bill of Materials (BOM) for this architecture is well within the project's budget. A representative BOM is outlined below.

| Component | Estimated Cost (INR) | Justification |
| :--- | :--- | :--- |
| ESP32 Development Board | ₹420 [[150](https://aiiottech.in/product-category/development-boards/)] | Provides low-cost, powerful processing with integrated Wi-Fi/BT. |
| ADXL345 Accelerometer Module | ₹150 [[25](https://electronicspices.com/product/adxl345-tripple-axis-accelerometer-board?srsltid=AfmBOooEwscfZjTJjN6A-rTWH--V7KDEVcR9AWUS3GTwgB2w9_MjVPby)] | Highly suitable, low-cost MEMS accelerometer with proven track record. |
| Small Enclosure / 3D Print File | ₹200 [[86](https://www.mdpi.com/1424-8220/23/24/9677)] | Protects electronics and aids in mechanical assembly. |
| Power Supply (e.g., 3.7V Li-ion Battery) | ₹300 [[178](https://www.call4electronics.in/electronic-components.html)] | Compact and portable power source. |
| Jumper Wires / PCB | ₹100 | Basic hardware for prototyping and connections. |
| **Total Estimated Cost** | **~₹1170** | **Significantly under the ₹3000–₹5000 budget, allowing for contingencies and optional upgrades.** |

The implementation of this architecture is deemed feasible by the five-member student team within a semester. The three members with stronger CS backgrounds can focus on the firmware development, leveraging the extensive Arduino/ESP32 ecosystem. The beginner programmer can be assigned manageable tasks like setting up the sensor readings or controlling status LEDs, fostering skill development. The remaining two members can concentrate on hardware sourcing, fabrication of the enclosure, mechanical assembly, and experimental testing. The primary engineering risks lie not in the novelty of the concept, but in the practical execution: ensuring a solid mechanical connection, debugging sensor communication, and characterizing the system's response to different abnormal conditions. These are tangible problems that can be solved through careful experimentation and iterative refinement.

In conclusion, the proposed architecture represents the strongest possible configuration to achieve the project's objectives. It is grounded in established engineering principles, uses mature and accessible components, and minimizes unnecessary complexity. It is robust, cost-effective, and realistically achievable by the student team. The architecture successfully validates the engineering hypothesis by providing a clear, reliable method for distinguishing normal from abnormal vibration using on-device intelligence. It is therefore **READY** to proceed into detailed engineering design and implementation.
